Chengzhi Liu<sup>1,2,\*</sup>, Yuxiang Lai<sup>1,6</sup>, Yuzhe Yang<sup>2</sup>, Yanqing Liu<sup>3</sup>, Jiheng Hou<sup>2</sup>, Yue Cao<sup>2</sup>, Haonan Wang<sup>7</sup>, Ruixi Fan<sup>1</sup>, Lan Wang<sup>1</sup>, Sheng Liu<sup>4</sup>, Songyou Peng<sup>1</sup>, Yale Song<sup>1</sup>, Kevin Qinghong Lin<sup>5</sup>, Xin Eric Wang<sup>2</sup>, Jui-Hsin Lai<sup>1</sup> and Youbao Tang<sup>1</sup>

<sup>1</sup> G<sub>oog</sub>l<sub>e,</sub> <sup>2</sup> U<sub>n</sub>i<sub>vers</sub>it<sub>y</sub> <sub>o</sub>f C<sub>a</sub>lif<sub>orn</sub>i<sub>a,</sub> S<sub>an</sub>t<sub>a</sub> B<sub>ar</sub>b<sub>ara,</sub> <sup>3</sup> U<sub>n</sub>i<sub>vers</sub>it<sub>y</sub> <sub>o</sub>f C<sub>a</sub>lif<sub>orn</sub>i<sub>a,</sub> S<sub>an</sub>t<sub>a</sub> C<sub>ruz,</sub> <sup>4</sup> St<sub>an</sub>f<sub>or</sub>d U<sub>n</sub>i<sub>vers</sub>it<sub>y,</sub> <sup>5</sup> U<sub>n</sub>i<sub>vers</sub>it<sub>y o</sub>f O<sub>x</sub>f<sub>or</sub>d<sub>,</sub> <sup>6</sup> E<sub>mory</sub> U<sub>n</sub>i<sub>vers</sub>it<sub>y,</sub> <sup>7</sup> C<sub>o</sub>l<sub>um</sub>bi<sub>a</sub> U<sub>n</sub>i<sub>vers</sub>it<sub>y</sub>

Vision-language models are shifting from ofline passive understanding toward online proactive interaction. However, prevailing methods largely rely on explicit requests while miss the implicit user intent (e.g.,“where should I click next?” while a user navigate a website), meanwhile fail to update the state simultaneously as user requests might update continuously. These limitations make them less suited to intent-aware interaction in practical. To address these challenges, we introduce StreamVibe-Bench, a new benchmark serving as testbed for user intent interaction, with 4.1K evaluation samples and 85K training samples spanning explicit, implicit, and dynamic intents. Building on this benchmark, we develop StreamVibe-VL, a streaming proactive model that infers implicit user intents from continuous observations and dynamically tailors its interactions as these intents evolve. We further introduce StreamVibe-Harness, which maintains user intents as persistent states with a fleixble lifecycles via a hierarchical memory to track the latest user intent and environmental state. Our experiments reveal three key findings: (i) StreamVibe-VL preserves strong general video understanding while improving response timing and answer quality by 10.7% over other baselines; (ii) existing proactive models remain limited in understanding user intent and deciding when to respond, especially under implicit and dynamic intents; and (iii) StreamVibe-Harness further improves response consistency and adaptation over sustained interaction, resulting in a 12.3% gain in proactive interaction.

This motivates three key research questions forward “Intent-aware interaction”:

How can we construct a testbed for proactive interaction with implicit user intent?

How can such intent-aware interaction be internalized as a native model capability?

How to flexible manage context to track environmental and user intent changes?

## 2. Related Work

Streaming Video Benchmark. Early video benchmarks focus on ofline understanding (Fu et al., 2025a; Zhao et al., 2025), while online benchmarks evaluate se<sub>q</sub>uential <sub>p</sub>erce<sub>p</sub>tion and res<sub>p</sub>onse before video com<sub>p</sub>letion (Huan<sub>g</sub> et al., 2025; Li et al., 2025b). Recent <sub>p</sub>roactive benchmarks further <sub>requ</sub>i<sub>re mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s</sub> t<sub>o</sub> d<sub>ec</sub>id<sub>e w</sub>h<sub>en</sub> t<sub>o respon</sub>d<sub>, o</sub>ft<sub>en us</sub>i<sub>ng anno</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>gger po</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>s an</sub>d <sub>ex</sub>t<sub>en</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> to e<sub>g</sub>ocentric or task-<sub>g</sub>uidance settin<sub>g</sub>s (Kundu et al., 2026; Ran et al., 2026; Wan<sub>g</sub> et al., 2025e). H<sub>owever, mos</sub>t f<sub>ocus on a s</sub>i<sub>ng</sub>l<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t t<sub>ype or env</sub>i<sub>ronmen</sub>t <sub>an</sub>d <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>t<sub>e responses</sub> i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>en</sub>tl<sub>y.</sub> St<sub>ream</sub>Vib<sub>e-</sub>B<sub>enc</sub>h i<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>ea</sub>d <sub>un</sub>ifi<sub>es exp</sub>li<sub>c</sub>it<sub>,</sub> i<sub>mp</sub>li<sub>c</sub>it<sub>, an</sub>d d<sub>ynam</sub>i<sub>c</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t<sub>s across p</sub>h<sub>ys</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>an</sub>d di<sub>g</sub>it<sub>a</sub>l tasks, with both pointwise and trajectory evaluation.

Proactive Interaction Models. Online video-language models support continuous perception and low-latenc<sub>y</sub> res<sub>p</sub>onses (Cui et al., 2026; Zen<sub>g</sub> et al., 2025), while real-time and task-<sub>g</sub>uidance s<sub>y</sub>stems further learn when to s<sub>p</sub>eak or <sub>p</sub>rovide assistance durin<sub>g</sub> on<sub>g</sub>oin<sub>g</sub> interaction (Liu et al., 2026; Lu et al., 2026; Siton<sub>g</sub> et al., 2026). However, most existin<sub>g</sub> a<sub>pp</sub>roaches center on ex<sub>p</sub>licitl<sub>y</sub> s<sub>p</sub>ecified re<sub>q</sub>uests or task objectives, with limited support for tracking interaction state and adapting to evolving user intent over time (Tack et al., 2026; Tao et al., 2026). StreamVibe-VL tar<sub>g</sub>ets o<sub>p</sub>en-ended <sub>p</sub>roactive i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erac</sub>ti<sub>on,</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t<sub>s</sub> <sub>mus</sub>t b<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>erre</sub>d f<sub>rom</sub> <sub>user</sub> <sub>goa</sub>l<sub>s,</sub> <sub>env</sub>i<sub>ronmen</sub>t <sub>c</sub>h<sub>anges,</sub> <sub>an</sub>d t<sub>as</sub>k <sub>progress,</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>up</sub>d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d th<sub>roug</sub>h<sub>ou</sub>t th<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erac</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub>

Memory and Agent Harness. Agentic systems increasingly incorporate persistent memory (Xu et al., 2025), <sub>p</sub>lannin<sub>g</sub> (Xion<sub>g</sub> et al., 2025), reflection (Yuan et al., 2025), and reusable skills (Zhen<sub>g</sub> et al., 2025) to <sub>p</sub>reserve and reuse historical context over lon<sub>g</sub>-horizon tasks. ## 3.2. Hierarchical Dificulty

StreamVibe-Bench characterizes proactive interaction along two dimensions: intent explicitness and intent dynamics. Existing benchmarks primarily focus on (i) Level 1: Explicit Intents, where the user <sub>spec</sub>ifi<sub>es</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>con</sub>diti<sub>on,</sub> <sub>even</sub>t<sub>,</sub> <sub>or</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>mon</sub>it<sub>or,</sub> <sub>an</sub>d th<sub>e</sub> <sub>agen</sub>t <sub>respon</sub>d<sub>s</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>en</sub> it <sub>occurs,</sub> <sub>e.g.</sub> L<sub>e</sub>t <sub>me</sub> k<sub>now w</sub>h<sub>en</sub> th<sub>e wa</sub>t<sub>er s</sub>t<sub>ar</sub>t<sub>s</sub> b<sub>o</sub>ili<sub>ng.</sub>

We further introduce (ii) Level 2: Implicit Intents, where the user provides only a high-level goal, <sub>requ</sub>i<sub>r</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>agen</sub>t t<sub>o</sub> i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>er</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>en</sub> <sub>an</sub>d h<sub>ow</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>ass</sub>i<sub>s</sub>t f<sub>rom</sub> <sub>ongo</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>o</sub>b<sub>serva</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> <sub>an</sub>d t<sub>as</sub>k <sub>progress,</sub> <sub>e.g.</sub> H<sub>e</sub>l<sub>p me</sub> k<sub>eep an eye on</sub> thi<sub>s</sub> di<sub>s</sub>h<sub>.</sub>

Finally, (iii) Level 3: Dynamic Intents considers intents that evolve throughout the interaction. As th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k <sub>un</sub>f<sub>o</sub>ld<sub>s,</sub> th<sub>e user may prov</sub>id<sub>e mu</sub>lti<sub>p</sub>l<sub>e new</sub> i<sub>npu</sub>t<sub>s, requ</sub>i<sub>r</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e agen</sub>t t<sub>o con</sub>ti<sub>nua</sub>ll<sub>y up</sub>d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e curren</sub>tl <sub>va</sub>lid t<sub>as</sub>k <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e.</sub>

We consider three common forms: Revision (RV), which modifies an existing intent; Composition (CP), which adds a new intent or constraint while preserving existing ones; and Interruption (IT), which i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>d<sub>uces an</sub> i<sub>mme</sub>di<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e reques</sub>t <sub>w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e ongo</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k <sub>rema</sub>i<sub>ns ac</sub>ti<sub>ve.</sub> Th<sub>ese c</sub>h<sub>anges may occur</sub> <sub>repea</sub>t<sub>e</sub>dl<sub>y</sub> <sub>an</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> <sub>com</sub>bi<sub>na</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>w</sub>ithi<sub>n</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>s</sub>i<sub>ng</sub>l<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erac</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub>

## 3.4. Data Statistics

Categories and Scale. Building on the three proactive interaction settings in Section 3.2, we organize StreamVibe-Bench into finer-grained task subtypes, as illustrated in Figure 2(b). The 600 Explicit Intents samples span Single Event (I), Transition (P), and Recurring Event (R). For Implicit Intents, 2,000 sam<sub>p</sub>les cover Guidance (G), Learnin<sub>g</sub> (L), Monitorin<sub>g</sub> (M), and Tool (T). The remainin<sub>g</sub> 1,500 Dynamic Intents samples consist of Revision (RV), Composition (CP), and Interruption (IT). ## 4.2. Evidence-Grounded Policy Learning for Streaming Interaction

Stage 1: Adaptive Streaming SFT. We introduce adaptive streaming supervision, which adjusts d<sub>ec</sub>i<sub>s</sub>i<sub>on-</sub>t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>en we</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht<sub>s</sub> t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e curren</sub>t t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> di<sub>s</sub>t<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on w</sub>hil<sub>e separa</sub>t<sub>e</sub>l<sub>y op</sub>ti<sub>m</sub>i<sub>z</sub>i<sub>ng response</sub>

![](images/7face23c4084c9a6ae6e0c2337c6e07a839a01b8b9e5540ef1890ad33bf45a4f.jpg)  
Figure 3 | Overview of StreamVibe. (a) During inference, StreamVibe-Harness maintains persistent i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erac</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e,</sub> t<sub>rac</sub>k<sub>s</sub> <sub>eac</sub>h i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t th<sub>roug</sub>h <sub>an</sub> i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>en</sub>t lif<sub>ecyc</sub>l<sub>e,</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>re</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>eves</sub> <sub>re</sub>l<sub>evan</sub>t hi<sub>s</sub>t<sub>ory</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> hi<sub>erarc</sub>hi<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>memory, prov</sub>idi<sub>ng</sub> St<sub>ream</sub>Vib<sub>e-</sub>VL <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>a un</sub>ifi<sub>e</sub>d <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ex</sub>t f<sub>or response</sub> d<sub>ec</sub>i<sub>s</sub>i<sub>ons.</sub> (b) Ada<sub>p</sub>tive streamin<sub>g</sub> SFT balances wait/s<sub>p</sub>eak decisions and jointl<sub>y</sub> su<sub>p</sub>ervises timin<sub>g</sub> and content. (c) Localized trajector<sub>y</sub> RL sam<sub>p</sub>les causal rollouts around hel<sub>p</sub> <sub>p</sub>oints, combinin<sub>g</sub> <sub>p</sub>oint-level timin<sub>g</sub> and content rewards with trajectory-level rewards on coverage, density, order, consistency, and <sub>re</sub>d<sub>un</sub>d<sub>ancy.</sub>

generation. This helps balance varying silence/response ratios across training sources and reduces bias toward excessive silence or over-response. Our SFT objective is

$$
\mathcal { L } _ { \mathrm { S F T } } = - \frac { 1 } { | \boldsymbol { y } | } \sum _ { j \in \boldsymbol { y } } \omega _ { j } \log \pi _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } \left( \boldsymbol { z } _ { j } \mid \boldsymbol { c } _ { t ( j ) } , \boldsymbol { z } _ { < j } \right) , \qquad \omega _ { j } = \left\{ \gamma _ { B , c ( j ) } , \quad j \in \mathcal { R } , \right. \mathrm { ~ }\tag{1}
$$

where Y denotes su<sub>p</sub>ervised assistant tokens, A and R denote decision and res<sub>p</sub>onse tokens, and $c ( j ) \in$ {response, silence} is the decision class. The adaptive weight $\gamma _ { c ( j ) } ^ { ( t ) }$ f<sub>o</sub>ll<sub>ows</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>curren</sub>t t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> di<sub>s</sub>t<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on,</sub> <sub>w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> $\lambda _ { \mathrm { t e x t } }$ p<sup>r</sup>ese<sup>rv</sup>es <sup>r</sup>espo<sup>n</sup>se<sup>-</sup>ge<sup>n</sup>e<sup>r</sup>a<sup>ti</sup>o<sup>n</sup> <sup>l</sup>ea<sup>rnin</sup>g.

Stage 2: Localized Trajectory RL. Building on SFT, we introduce localized trajectory reinforcement learning. Full-trajectory optimization mixes correct and erroneous behaviors, leading to difuse credit <sub>ass</sub>i<sub>gnmen</sub>t <sub>an</sub>d <sub>rewar</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>er</sub>f<sub>erence.</sub> W<sub>e</sub> th<sub>ere</sub>f<sub>ore cons</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t <sub>s</sub>h<sub>or</sub>t <sub>causa</sub>l <sub>ro</sub>ll<sub>ou</sub>t<sub>s aroun</sub>d <sub>anno</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d hel<sub>p</sub> <sub>p</sub>oints and o<sub>p</sub>timize them with GRPO (Shao et al., 2024) usin<sub>g</sub> com<sub>p</sub>lementar<sub>y</sub> <sub>p</sub>oint-level and trajectory-level rewards.

(i) Point-Level Reward. For each evidence point � and predicted response �, the temporal ofset i<sub>s</sub> d<sub>e</sub>fi<sub>ne</sub>d <sub>as</sub> $\Delta _ { g , p } = t _ { p } - t _ { g }$ <sub>.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> ti<sub>m</sub>i<sub>ng score</sub> $\tau ( \Delta _ { g , p } ) \in [ 0 , 1 ]$ <sub>measures a</sub>li<sub>gnmen</sub>t <sub>w</sub>ithi<sub>n an asym-</sub> <sub>me</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>c response w</sub>i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>ow, w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> $\rho ( g , p ) \in [ 0 , 1 ]$ measures LLM-judged content quality. Their product $\tau ( \Delta _ { g , p } ) \rho ( g , p )$ serves as the matchin<sub>g</sub> score, and one-to-one matchin<sub>g</sub> <sub>y</sub>ields the set M, where each <sub>ev</sub>id<sub>ence po</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t <sub>an</sub>d <sub>response can</sub> b<sub>e ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d <sub>a</sub>t <sub>mos</sub>t <sub>once.</sub> Th<sub>e ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d <sub>scores are</sub> th<sub>en aggrega</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> <sub>accoun</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> f<sub>or</sub> <sub>m</sub>i<sub>sse</sub>d <sub>ev</sub>id<sub>ence</sub> <sub>po</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>s</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>excess</sub>i<sub>ve</sub> <sub>responses.</sub>

$$
R _ { \mathrm { a l i g n } } = \frac { ( 1 + \alpha ) \sum _ { ( g , p ) \in M } \tau ( \Delta _ { g , p } ) \rho ( g , p ) } { N _ { \mathrm { p r e d } } + \alpha N _ { \mathrm { g t } } }\tag{2}
$$

Here, $N _ { \mathrm { p r e d } }$ <sub>an</sub>d $N _ { \mathrm { g t } }$ d<sub>eno</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>num</sub>b<sub>ers</sub> <sub>o</sub>f <sub>pre</sub>di<sub>c</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>responses</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>ev</sub>id<sub>ence</sub> <sub>po</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>s,</sub> <sub>respec</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub>l<sub>y,</sub> <sub>w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> <sub>�</sub> <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>l<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>ra</sub>d<sub>e-o</sub>f b<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ween</sub> <sub>m</sub>i<sub>sse</sub>d <sub>ev</sub>id<sub>ence</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>over-response.</sub>

(ii) Trajectory-Level reward. We further introduce a trajectory-level reward $R _ { \mathrm { g l o b } }$ t<sub>o eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>t<sub>e eac</sub>h <sub>ro</sub>ll<sub>ou</sub>t <sub>as a w</sub>h<sub>o</sub>l<sub>e.</sub> Si<sub>nce po</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>-</sub>l<sub>eve</sub>l <sub>rewor</sub>d <sub>a</sub>l<sub>one</sub> d<sub>oes no</sub>t <sub>ensure co</sub>h<sub>eren</sub>t i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erac</sub>ti<sub>on,</sub> $R _ { \mathrm { g l o b } }$ <sub>cap</sub>t<sub>ures</sub> th<sub>e overa</sub>ll <sub>cons</sub>i<sub>s</sub>t<sub>ency an</sub>d <sub>na</sub>t<sub>ura</sub>l<sub>ness o</sub>f th<sub>e response sequence,</sub> di<sub>scourag</sub>i<sub>ng re</sub>d<sub>un</sub>d<sub>an</sub>t <sub>or con</sub>fli<sub>c</sub>ti<sub>ng responses an</sub>d f<sub>avor</sub>i<sub>ng co</sub>h<sub>eren</sub>t <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ream</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> b<sub>e</sub>h<sub>av</sub>i<sub>or.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>na</sub>l <sub>rewar</sub>d i<sub>s</sub> d<sub>e</sub>fi<sub>ne</sub>d <sub>as</sub> $R = \lambda R _ { \mathrm { a l i g n } } + ( 1 - \lambda ) R _ { \mathrm { g l o b } }$ <sub>, w</sub>ith $\lambda = 0 . 7$ t<sub>o p</sub>l<sub>ace grea</sub>t<sub>er emp</sub>h<sub>as</sub>i<sub>s on response</sub> ti<sub>m</sub>i<sub>ng an</sub>d <sub>con</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t quality while preserving trajectory-level coherence. Since response formatting is already well learned during SFT, the RL stage optimizes only these two objectives without introducing an additional format <sub>rewar</sub>d<sub>.</sub>

## 5. Experiments

I<sub>n</sub> thi<sub>s</sub> <sub>sec</sub>ti<sub>on,</sub> <sub>we</sub> <sub>con</sub>d<sub>uc</sub>t <sub>ex</sub>t<sub>ens</sub>i<sub>ve</sub> <sub>exper</sub>i<sub>men</sub>t<sub>s</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>answer</sub> th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>o</sub>ll<sub>ow</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>researc</sub>h <sub>ques</sub>ti<sub>ons:</sub>

♠ RQ1: How top-performing baseline behave and can StreamVibe-VL outperform them in intent-aware i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erac</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>se</sub>tti<sub>ng</sub>?

♠ RQ2: What limitations do current proactive agents exhibit in sustained proactive interaction?

♠ RQ3: What gains does the StreamVibe-Harness provide beyond the end-to-end policy?

## 5.1. Setup

Benchmarks. We evaluate StreamVibe across two settings: (i) Proactive Interaction on StreamVibe-Bench, General Timin<sub>g</sub> (Yao et al., 2026b), E<sub>g</sub>oProactive (Kundu et al., 2026), and IPIBench (Li et al., 2026b); and (ii) General Video Understanding on MVBench (Li et al., 2024), Video-MME (Fu et al., 2025a), OVO-Bench (Li et al., 2025b), and OVBench (Huan<sub>g</sub> et al., 2025). External suites retain th<sub>e</sub>i<sub>r o</sub>fi<sub>c</sub>i<sub>a</sub>l <sub>pro</sub>t<sub>oco</sub>l<sub>s.</sub> F<sub>ur</sub>th<sub>er</sub> d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>a</sub>il<sub>s are prov</sub>id<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> A<sub>ppen</sub>di<sub>x</sub> A<sub>.</sub>

Baselines. We benchmark StreamVibe against representative models from the three categories used in Table 2: (i) Proprietary Models, including Gemini-3.1-Pro and Gemini-3.8-Flash (Google DeepMind, 2026), and GPT-Realtime-2.1 (OpenAI, 2026); (ii) Open-Source Ofline Models, including Qwen3- VL-8B (Bai et al., 2025), Qwen3.5-9B (Qwen Team, 2026), and Jo<sub>y</sub>AI-VL-Interaction (Yao et al., 2026b); and (iii) Open-Source Streaming Models, including MMDuet2 (Wang et al., 2025d), Mage-VL (Yan<sub>g</sub> et al., 2026d), LiveStar (Yan<sub>g</sub> et al., 2025), ViS<sub>p</sub>eak (Fu et al., 2025b), and VideoLLM-Online (Chen et al., 2024).

Evaluation Settings. For StreamVibe-Bench, we use the protocol and metrics defined in Section 3.5, with Claude Sonnet 4.6 (Anthro<sub>p</sub>ic, 2026) as a shared jud<sub>g</sub>e and res<sub>p</sub>onse <sub>q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub> scored on a 0–4 <sub>sca</sub>l<sub>e.</sub> F<sub>or ex</sub>t<sub>erna</sub>l b<sub>enc</sub>h<sub>mar</sub>k<sub>s, we s</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>c</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> f<sub>o</sub>ll<sub>ow</sub> th<sub>e</sub>i<sub>r o</sub>fi<sub>c</sub>i<sub>a</sub>l <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>ti<sub>on pro</sub>t<sub>oco</sub>l<sub>s an</sub>d <sub>me</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>cs.</sub> S<sub>ee</sub> A<sub>ppen</sub>di<sub>x</sub> B<sub>.</sub>5 f<sub>or eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>a</sub>il<sub>s.</sub>

B<sub>u</sub>ildi<sub>ng on</sub> th<sub>e overa</sub>ll <sub>exper</sub>i<sub>men</sub>t<sub>a</sub>l <sub>resu</sub>lt<sub>s a</sub>b<sub>ove, we</sub> f<sub>ur</sub>th<sub>er ana</sub>l<sub>yze per</sub>f<sub>ormance across</sub> dif<sub>eren</sub>t <sub>su</sub>bt<sub>ypes</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>o</sub>bt<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>more</sub> fi<sub>ne-gra</sub>i<sub>ne</sub>d <sub>un</sub>d<sub>ers</sub>t<sub>an</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> <sub>o</sub>f <sub>proac</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erac</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>capa</sub>biliti<sub>es.</sub> S<sub>pec</sub>ifi<sub>ca</sub>ll<sub>y,</sub> we examine (i) the relative dificulty of diferent subtypesfor existing systems, (ii) how model performance changes across evaluation settings, and (iii) how RL shifts response behavior and performance trade-ofs across subtypes. All analyses use a matching tolerance of ±2 s. Figures 8–10 report subtype-level results under the Pointwise and Trajectory evaluation settings used in Tables 3 and 7. Figure 8 <sub>summar</sub>i<sub>zes</sub> th<sub>e average per</sub>f<sub>ormance o</sub>f <sub>a</sub>ll <sub>ava</sub>il<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s across su</sub>bt<sub>ypes un</sub>d<sub>er eac</sub>h <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>pro</sub>t<sub>oco</sub>l t<sub>o c</sub>h<sub>arac</sub>t<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ze</sub> th<sub>e overa</sub>ll difi<sub>cu</sub>lt<sub>y o</sub>f dif<sub>eren</sub>t t<sub>as</sub>k t<sub>ypes.</sub> P<sub>o</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>w</sub>i<sub>se</sub> i<sub>nc</sub>l<sub>u</sub>d<sub>es seven mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s,</sub> while Trajectory additionally includes five native streaming baselines.

Trajectory evaluation reveals substantially greater dificulty under sustained interaction. As shown in Figure 8, performance drops markedly from Pointwise to Trajectory evaluation across all <sub>su</sub>bt<sub>ypes.</sub> U<sub>n</sub>d<sub>er</sub> P<sub>o</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>w</sub>i<sub>se eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>ti<sub>on,</sub> Ti<sub>m</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> F1 <sub>rema</sub>i<sub>ns re</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub>l<sub>y</sub> hi<sub>g</sub>h <sub>a</sub>t 63<sub>.</sub>5<sub>–</sub>75<sub>.</sub>6<sub>,</sub> i<sub>n</sub>di<sub>ca</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s can o</sub>ft<sub>en recogn</sub>i<sub>ze</sub> l<sub>oca</sub>ll<sub>y appropr</sub>i<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e response momen</sub>t<sub>s w</sub>h<sub>en eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>a</sub>t i<sub>so</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d timestamps. In contrast, Trajectory Timing F1 falls to 12.1–28.2 and Content-F1 to 2.7–11.1, revealing <sub>a su</sub>b<sub>s</sub>t<sub>an</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>l <sub>gap</sub> b<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ween</sub> l<sub>oca</sub>l <sub>percep</sub>ti<sub>on an</sub>d <sub>sus</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>ne</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erac</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> Thi<sub>s sugges</sub>t<sub>s</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>ma</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> reliable assistance over a full trajectory requires more than recognizing individual events: models <sub>mus</sub>t <sub>con</sub>ti<sub>nuous</sub>l<sub>y</sub> <sub>up</sub>d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>ac</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t<sub>s,</sub> t<sub>rac</sub>k t<sub>as</sub>k <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e,</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>regu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>responses</sub> b<sub>ase</sub>d <sub>on</sub> th<sub>e</sub>i<sub>r</sub> <sub>evo</sub>l<sub>v</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erac</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> hi<sub>s</sub>t<sub>ory.</sub>

Fi<sub>gure</sub> 8 f<sub>ur</sub>th<sub>er s</sub>h<sub>ows</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>su</sub>bt<sub>ype</sub> difi<sub>cu</sub>lt<sub>y c</sub>h<sub>anges un</sub>d<sub>er sus</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>ne</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erac</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> F<sub>or</sub> E<sub>xp</sub>li<sub>c</sub>it intents, I achieves the highest Pointwise Timing F1 at 75.6 but the lowest Trajectory Timing F1 at 14<sub>.</sub>5<sub>, w</sub>h<sub>ereas</sub> R <sub>reac</sub>h<sub>es</sub> 28<sub>.</sub>1<sub>.</sub> A<sub>mong</sub> I<sub>mp</sub>li<sub>c</sub>it i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t<sub>s,</sub> T<sub>oo</sub>l/GUI <sub>rema</sub>i<sub>ns re</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub>l<sub>y s</sub>t<sub>rong, w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> G<sub>u</sub>id<sub>ance</sub> b<sub>ecomes more c</sub>h<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>eng</sub>i<sub>ng.</sub> F<sub>or</sub> D<sub>ynam</sub>i<sub>c</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t<sub>s,</sub> R<sub>ev</sub>i<sub>s</sub>i<sub>on an</sub>d C<sub>ompos</sub>iti<sub>on un</sub>d<sub>erper</sub>f<sub>orm</sub> I<sub>n</sub>t<sub>errup</sub>ti<sub>on,</sub> hi<sub>g</sub>hli<sub>g</sub>hti<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e</sub> difi<sub>cu</sub>lt<sub>y o</sub>f <sub>con</sub>ti<sub>nuous</sub>l<sub>y rev</sub>i<sub>s</sub>i<sub>ng an</sub>d <sub>compos</sub>i<sub>ng ac</sub>ti<sub>ve requ</sub>i<sub>remen</sub>t<sub>s.</sub> Overall, Pointwise evaluation primarily reflects local recognition and timing, whereas Trajectory <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>exposes</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>a</sub>dditi<sub>ona</sub>l <sub>c</sub>h<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>enges</sub> <sub>o</sub>f <sub>ma</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>co</sub>h<sub>eren</sub>t <sub>an</sub>d <sub>re</sub>li<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> b<sub>e</sub>h<sub>av</sub>i<sub>or</sub> th<sub>roug</sub>h<sub>ou</sub>t <sub>con</sub>ti<sub>nuous, evo</sub>l<sub>v</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erac</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub>

Acceptance <sub>requ</sub>i<sub>res</sub> f<sub>our</sub> i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>en</sub>t <sub>approva</sub>l<sub>s o</sub>f th<sub>e same samp</sub>l<sub>e vers</sub>i<sub>on, w</sub>ith <sub>no unreso</sub>l<sub>ve</sub>d i<sub>ssue.</sub>
<table><tr><td>Criterion</td><td>Standard for approval</td></tr><tr><td>Intent fidelity</td><td>The instruction is coherent and compatible with the task. The assigned Explicit, Implicit, or Dynamic category matches the interaction; inferred intents are supported by the user&#x27;s context, not invented.</td></tr><tr><td>Evidence and causality</td><td>Every annotated intervention has identifiable visual or interaction evidence available by its response time. Future frames and later user turns are not needed to</td></tr><tr><td>Coverage and non-redundancy</td><td>justify the response. Distinct, necessary intervention opportunities are covered, without duplicate evidence points or repeated assistance after the relevant intent has already been</td></tr><tr><td>Temporal validity</td><td>satisfied. Each response window and preferred timestamp correspond to a moment when the</td></tr><tr><td>Reference-answer quality</td><td>evidence is sufficient and assistance is still useful; the preferred time lies within the window and neither anticipates evidence nor misses the actionable moment. The reference is correct, relevant, and useful for the active intent. It avoids unsupported details, contradictory advice, and unnecessary instructions, while</td></tr><tr><td>Dynamic-intent consistency</td><td>remaining consistent with the visible state. For Dynamic samples, timestamped revisions, compositions, and interruptions are coherent. References follow the latest valid intent and preserve unaffected constraints without acting on superseded ones; this criterion is not applicable to</td></tr><tr><td>Record integrity</td><td>other categories. The video, source identifier, user-turn timestamps, evidence windows, and reference answers are complete and aligned. Each decision refers to an identifiable sample version.</td></tr></table>

Independent agreement audit. Unanimous acceptance is a quality-control rule, not itself evidence of high inter-annotator agreement. The agreement audit must use the original, independent judgments on a fixed set of candidate samples before discussion, correction, or acceptance filtering, including <sub>can</sub>did<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>are</sub> <sub>no</sub>t <sub>re</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>ne</sub>d<sub>.</sub> E<sub>ac</sub>h <sub>rev</sub>i<sub>ewer</sub> <sub>supp</sub>li<sub>es</sub> <sub>a</sub> bi<sub>nary</sub> d<sub>ec</sub>i<sub>s</sub>i<sub>on,</sub> <sub>approve</sub> <sub>or</sub> <sub>requ</sub>i<sub>re</sub> revision/reject, under the same rubric. Analysis. As shown in Table 17, human–human Fleiss’ � ranges from 0.82 to 0.87 across all categories, fallin<sub>g</sub> within the “almost <sub>p</sub>erfect a<sub>g</sub>reement” band (>0.81) (Landis and Koch, 1977), indicatin<sub>g</sub> that the four reviewers maintained highly consistent judgments under the shared rubric. Explicit achieves the hi<sub>g</sub>hest a<sub>g</sub>reement (0.87), while D<sub>y</sub>namic is sli<sub>g</sub>htl<sub>y</sub> lower (0.82), consistent with the inherentl<sub>y</sub> <sub>g</sub>reater com<sub>p</sub>lexit<sub>y</sub> of annotatin<sub>g</sub> evolvin<sub>g</sub> intents. Gemini–human Cohen’s <sub>�</sub> ran<sub>g</sub>es from 0.73 to 0.79<sub>,</sub> within the “substantial a<sub>g</sub>reement” band (0.61–0.80), with narrow <sub>p</sub>er-<sub>p</sub>air ran<sub>g</sub>es, indicatin<sub>g</sub> that Gemini’s review decisions are well aligned with human judgments.
